Constructing Invariant and Equivariant Operations by Symmetric Tensor Network
Meng Zhang, Chao Wang, Hao Zhang, Shaojun Dong, Lixin He

Abstract
Design of neural networks that incorporate symmetry is crucial for geometric deep learning. Central to this effort is the development of invariant and equivariant operations. This works presents a systematic method for constructing valid invariant and equivariant operations. It can handle inputs and outputs in the form of Cartesian tensors with different rank, as well as spherical tensors with different types. In addition, our method features a graphical representation utilizing the symmetric tensor network, which simplifies both the proofs and constructions related to invariant and equivariant functions. We also apply this approach to design the equivariant interaction message for the geometry graph neural network, and equivariant machine learning model to learn the constitutive law of materials.



1
Introduction

Many scientific problems involve systems with 3D geometric structures, such as molecules and materials. For these systems, we can always describe them using 3D coordinates. However, physical quantities in the natural world do not depend on any specific coordinate system. They exhibit invariance or equivariance under changes of coordinates, such as rotations and translations. Therefore, when performing machine learning tasks involving physical quantities, it is advantageous in data efficiency and generalization to incorporate invariance or equivariance into the hypothesis neural networks
[
1
]
.

Designing the invariant or equivariant operations in the neural networks is crucial step in developing the invariance/equivariance machine learning models. For equivariant geometry graph neural networks (EGNN) with vector features, the equivariant operations on vector features are typically vector summation
v_{1}+v_{2}

[
2
,
3
,
4
]
and vector product
v_{1}\times v_{2}

[
5
]
. For EGNN with higher-type (order) spherical tensors features, the equivariant operations most used are the tensor product (TP) operations
[
6
,
7
]
. In addition, recent works also use the higher-rank Cartesian tensors as the equivariant feature in the message passing, in which the typical equivariant operations used are tensor contraction and summation of tensors
[
8
]
.

In this work, we developed a systematic tool capable of constructing
SO(3)
invariance and equivariance operations given the specified forms of input and output, which include tuples of various rank Cartesian tensors and various types spherical tensors. The main tool we used is the symmetric tensor network
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
, which is wildly used in quantum many-body systems. Combining the classical invariance theory
[
12
]
, we developed a framework for building generators of invariant functions, which we call tensor network generators, designed for a specific input format. We can obtain the equivariant operations corresponding to specified input and output quantity forms by calculating the derivatives of the tensor network generators. Based on the method we developed, we demonstrate how it can be used to construct invariant and equivariant operators in geometric graph neural networks. Furthermore, we also construct the equivariant machine learning model to learn the constitutive law of material utilizing the method we developed in this work.

2
Related work

Invariant and equivariant operations
: For geometry graph neural networks, previous works vary in the choice of invariant and equivariant operations base on different input/output features including 3D vectors
[
2
,
3
,
4
]
, higher-type spherical tensors
[
5
,
6
,
13
]
and higher-rank Cartesian tensors
[
8
]
. Works
[
14
,
15
]
show to how to construct the
O(N)
invariant and equivariant function for Cartesian tensors in theory based on Weyl’s classical invariance theory
[
12
]
. Furthermore, works
[
16
]
shows how to get equivariant functions from the derivative of invariant polynomials base on the method of B. Malgrange.

Tensor network:
Early contributions
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
described how to incorporate the
SU(2)
symmetry for tensor networks states for quantum many-body systems. More recently, Works
[
17
]
use the fusion diagram (a graphical representation of the Clebsh-Gordan decomposition) to construct the
SO(3)
equivariant blocks. Works
[
18
]
used the symmetric tensor network for constructing the machine-learning interatomic potentials. In addition, works
[
19
]
shows how to construct the
O(N)
invariant and equivariant functions for Cartesian tensors by tensor network.

3
Preliminaries

3.1
Tensor network

The tensor networks have been proved to be a powerful mathematical framework and computational tool across multiple disciplines in recent years. These structured decompositions of high-dimensional tensors into networks of lower-dimensional tensors were originally developed in the context of quantum many-body physics
[
20
,
21
,
22
]
, but have since found widespread applications in machine learning
[
23
,
24
,
25
,
26
]
, quantum computing
[
27
,
28
]
, applied mathematics
[
29
]
, and beyond.

We first introduce the formalism of tensor, which is the building blocks of tensor network. A tensor
T
is a multi-dimensional array. We can donate its elements as
T_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}}\in\mathbb{R}^{I_{1}\times I_{2}\times\cdots\times I_{n}}
, where the
n
is the rank of the tensor and
I_{k}
is the dimension size of index
i_{k}
. The tensor also has a graphical representation. As shown in Fig.
1
(a), a tensor can be represented by a node with legs, where each leg correspond
to a index of the tensor. A vector can be represented by a one-leg node and a matrix an be represented by a two-leg node.

A tensor network is a collection of tensors defines above. The legs connected between nodes are the indices
needed to be summed over, which is called contraction. Therefore, the tensor network can be contracted to a single tensor, the index of which correspond to the open leg of the tensor network. As shown in Fig.
1
(b), the tensor network describes the contraction of tensor
A
and
B
i.e. matrix multiplication. Furthermore, we can also give the graphical representation of the derivative of the tensor network. As shown in Fig.
1
(c), the derivative of the a tensor network with respect to a specific tensor
T
(appears only once in the network) is a tensor network where tensor
T
is removed.

Actually, a tensor network represents a certain decomposition of a high rank tensor. As shown in Fig.
1
(d), a rank-N tensor is decomposed to rank-2 an rank-3 tensors, where this decomposition is called tensor train decomposition
[
29
]
(or matrix product state
[
20
,
21
]
in quantum many-body physics community).

Figure 1:
(a) Graphical representation of rank-1 tensor (vector) and rank-2 tensor (matrix). (b) The contraction of tensor
A
and
B
, this is the matrix multiplication
C_{ik}=\sum_{j}A_{ij}B_{jk}
. (c) The derivative of the a tensor network with respect to a specific tensor
T
, the results of which is a tensor network where tensor
T
is removed. (d) Tensor train decomposition, namely, a rank-N tensor is decomposed to rank-2 an rank-3 tensors.

3.2
Group invariance and equivariance

We can give the definition of group invariance and equivariance functions as follows:

Definition 3.1
.

Let
G
be a group which acts on linear spaces
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
over field
F
by certain linear representation.
An invariant function
f:\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\rightarrow F
in a multi-variable function such that for each
g\in G
,

f(g\cdot\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,g\cdot\bm{x}_{n})=f(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})

(1)

Definition 3.2
.

Let
G
be a group which acts on linear spaces
V_{1},\dots,V_{n},U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
over
F
by certain linear representation. An equivariant function
f:\oplus_{i}V_{i}\rightarrow\oplus_{i}U_{i}
in a multi-variable function such that for each
g\in G
,

f^{i}(g\cdot\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,g\cdot\textbf{}x_{n})=g\cdot f^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})

(2)

where the
\cdot
mean the group action on linear space. For example, the inputs of the equivariance functions can be 3D coordinates of each atom in a molecule, and the outputs can be the force of each atom. In the remaining part of the paper, we mainly focus on
SO(3)
group and are restricted to the real case
F=\mathbb{R}
.

3.3
Symmetric tensor network

A symmetric tensor
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
is a tensor that is invariant under a group action in the space of each of its index.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\in\mathbb{R}^{I_{1}\times\cdots\times I_{n}}
be a tensor, and
\rho_{i}:G\mapsto GL(I_{i},\mathbb{R})
be the group representation on space of
i
-th index.
(T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}},(\rho_{1},\dots))
is call a (group)
symmetric tensor
iff

\forall g\in G:\prod_{i}\rho_{i}(g)_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}T_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}=T_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}}

(3)

This can be illustrated by Fig.
2
(a). A symmetric tensor network is a collection of symmetric tensors defines above. Fig.
2
(b) provides a graphical representation that tensor networks that consist of symmetric tensors is also a symmetric tensors as a whole. Thus, the contraction operation preserves tensor symmetry
[
9
]
. This fundamental property allows us to employ a more restrictive formation for tensors with predefined symmetries, namely, constructing the tensor network exclusively from symmetric tensors
[
9
]
. We give more details about the symmetric tensor network in the Appendix
A
.

Figure 2:
Symmetric tensors and symmetric tensor networks.
(a) The graphical illustration of the equation
\forall g\in G:\prod_{i}\rho_{i}(g)_{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}}T_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}=T_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}}
.
(b) Tensor networks that consist of symmetric tensors is also a symmetric tensors as a whole. We fist insert identity
\rho_{i}(g)\rho_{i}(g)^{T}=I
on the contracted leg. Due to every tensor in the network is symmetric, the tensor networks as a whole is also symmetric.

4
Tensor network generators

The polynomials of
n
variables input
{\bm{x}}=(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n})
, denoted by
F[V]
, actually forms a mathematical structure called algebra. It’s easy to see that the invariant polynomials forms a subalgebra denoted by
F[V]^{G}
. In other words, the subset of invariant polynomials is a subspace closed under multiplication. By Hilbert’s finiteness theorem
[
30
,
31
]
,
F[V]^{G}
is finitely generated when
G
is finite group or compact Lie group (including the case that
G=SO(3)
). In other words, any invariant polynomial
f
takes the form of

f(\bm{x})=q(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})

(4)

where
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}\in F[V]^{G}
, and
q
is a general polynomial. Here, the polynomials
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
are not dependent on
f
. They are called generators of
F[V]^{G}
. As a result, any invariant polynomial of
{\bm{x}}
can be expressed by polynomial of
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
. More generally, we may approximate invariant function by invariant polynomial
[
32
]
.Therefore, an invariant function can be approximated by a function of generators
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
. In the following, we restrict to the special case
F=\mathbb{R}
and
G=SO(3)
. We’ll give a systematic method to construct the generators of
\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
by tensor network, which we call tensor network generators.

4.1
Vector inputs

Firstly, let’s consider the simplest case, where the input are 3D vectors
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In this case, the input space is
V=\mathbb{R}^{3n}
. Weyl
[
12
]
proved that

Lemma 4.1
.

The set of generators of
\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
is
\{\bm{x}_{i}\cdot\bm{x}_{j},(\bm{x}_{i}\times\bm{x}_{j})\cdot\bm{x}_{k}\}
.
Therefore any invariant polynomial takes the form of

f(\bm{x})=q(\{\bm{x}_{i}\cdot\bm{x}_{j},(\bm{x}_{i}\times\bm{x}_{j})\cdot\bm{x}_{k}\})

(5)

This lemma not only provides a finite set of generators of 3D vector inputs, but can also be used to greatly simplify the structure of a symmetric tensor, which is useful in treating the other input form, which we discuss later.

Lemma 4.2
.

Each
SO(3)
symmetric tensor
T\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3\times\cdots\times 3}
is generated by identity tensor
\delta_{ij}
and Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{ijk}
. That is to say, each
SO(3)
symmetric tensor
T
is linear combination of tensors each of which is the tensor product of
\delta_{ij}
and
\epsilon_{ijk}
.

Furthermore, if the rank of
T
is even, then
T
is linear combination of tensors each of which is the tensor product of
\delta_{ij}
. Otherwise,
T
is linear combination of tensors each of which is the tensor product of
\delta_{ij}
together with exactly one
\epsilon_{ijk}
.

Proof.

Let
T
be an
SO(3)
-symmetric tensor whose indices take the 3D representation of
SO(3)
, and
\bm{x}_{i}
be variables each in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We can define a
SO(3)
-symmetric polynomial

f(\bm{x})=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}T_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}(\bm{x}_{1})_{i_{1}}\cdots(\bm{x}_{n})_{i_{n}}

(6)

By Lemma 4.1, we have
f(\bm{x})=\sum_{i}c_{i}p_{i}

where each
p_{i}
is product of elements in
\{\bm{x}_{i}\cdot\bm{x}_{j},(\bm{x}_{i}\times\bm{x}_{j})\cdot\bm{x}_{k}\}
and
c_{i}
is the coefficients. Taking derivative of
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
on both sides, we can see that
T
is of the form of finite sum
T=\sum_{i}c_{i}T_{i}
, where
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and each
T_{i}
is the tensor product of
\delta_{ij}
and
\epsilon_{ijk}
.

Considering the parity of the rank, there is odd (even)
\epsilon_{ijk}
in each
T_{i}
if the rank of
T
is odd (even). Notice that

\epsilon_{ijk}\epsilon_{lmn}=\delta_{il}(\delta_{jm}\delta_{kn}-\delta_{jn}\delta_{km})-\delta_{im}(\delta_{jl}\delta_{kn}-\delta_{jn}\delta_{kl})+\delta_{in}(\delta_{jl}\delta_{km}-\delta_{jm}\delta_{kl})

(7)

Then tensor product of odd number of
\epsilon_{ijk}
reduces to one
\epsilon_{ijk}
. The tensor product of even number of
\epsilon_{ijk}
reduces to product and sum of the tensor
\delta_{ij}
.
∎

The symmetric tensor
\delta_{ij}
and
\epsilon_{ijk}
can be represent by

\epsilon_{i}{}_{j}{}_{k}:
\delta_{i}{}_{j}:

(8)

It should be noted that these
SO(3)
symmetric tensors whose indices take the 3D representation, along with their characteristic properties, are also known as isotropic tensors
[
33
]
in the classical invariance theory.

4.2
Cartesian tensor inputs

Next, let’s consider a more general case, where the input
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3\times\cdots\times 3}
are Cartesian tensors with various rank whose indices take the 3D representation of
SO(3)
. For this case, we can construct the tensor network generators as following way,

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
be input Cartesian tensors whose indices take the 3D representation of SO(3). Let
V
be the input space. Then
\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
is generated by the contraction of connected tensor network formed by
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
(multiplicity is allowed) together with identity tensor
\delta_{ij}
and at most one Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{ijk}
.

The proof of Theorem
4.3
is given in Appendix
B
.

When all inputs
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
are vectors, the tensor network generators are
\{\bm{x}_{i}\cdot\bm{x}_{j},(\bm{x}_{i}\times\bm{x}_{j})\cdot\bm{x}_{k}\}
, which is the same as in Lemma
5
. In this case, it’s easy to see that
\{\bm{x}_{i}\cdot\bm{x}_{j},(\bm{x}_{i}\times\bm{x}_{j})\cdot\bm{x}_{k}\}
is a minimal set of generator. However, if the inputs
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
contains tensor of higher rank, the tensor-network generators are not minimal. Since by Hilbert’s finiteness theorem
[
30
,
31
]
, we have a finite set of generator
(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})
which are generated by a finite subset of tensor-network generators. That means only a finite subset of tensor-network generators suffices to generate the whole
\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
. However, it’s very difficult to determine the exact subset. In practical, we can take the subset of tensor network polynomials with degree
\leq D
, which gives a finite-degree approximation to the minimal set of generators. We also noted that resent work
[
15
]
construct the equivariant polynomial function with Cartesian tensor inputs in similar way, which use group averaging property of the orthogonal groups
[
33
]
.

4.3
Spherical tensor inputs

Irreducible representations of
SO(3)
group can be labeled by a non-negative integer
l
. The representation
l
is
(2l+1)
-dimensional, and the common 3D representation is just the representation
1
. In this section, we consider inputs
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
of irreducible representation spaces, that is, each
\bm{x}_{i}\in V_{i}
where
V_{i}
is a real linear space of an irreducible
SO(3)
representation
l_{i}
.

A rank-
r
tensor whose indices take the 3D representation of
SO(3)
is a reducible representation and can be reduced as follows.

(1)^{\otimes l}=(l)\oplus(l-1)^{d_{l,l-1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus(s)^{d_{l,s}}\oplus\cdots\oplus(0)^{d_{l,0}}

(9)

where
(1)^{\otimes l}=\underbrace{(1)\otimes\cdots\otimes(1)}_{l}
and
(s)^{d}=\underbrace{(s)\oplus\cdots\oplus(s)}_{d}
. The value of
d_{l,s}
is given in Appendix
C
.

For each representation
l
, we can define an
SO(3)
-symmetric tensor
P_{l}
which projects from the space
(1)^{\otimes l}
to the space
l
. The exact form we constructed of
P_{l}
is given in Appendix
D
.
For
P_{l}
, we have

(11)

Then
P_{l}(\bm{x})
becomes a tensor
(1)^{\otimes l}
, which can be used to construct tensor network generators.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
be input variables that take the irreducible representation
l_{1},\dots,l_{n}
of SO(3). Let
V
be the input space. Then
\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
is generated by the contraction of connected tensor network formed by
P_{l_{1}}(\bm{x}_{1}),\dots,P_{l_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
(multiplicity is allowed) together with at most one Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{ijk}
.

The proof is given in Appendix
E
.

4.4
General tensor inputs

In the most general case, we would expect that the inputs
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
are vectors in
SO(3)
of general representation spaces. For example,
\bm{x}_{i}
may be of representation
(1)\oplus(3)\oplus(7)
. The construction of tensor network generators is similar to the last section of spherical tensor inputs, except for each
\bm{x}_{i}
(which is of representation
\omega_{i}
), we should choose projector
P_{\omega_{i}}
which projects from the space
(1)^{\otimes r_{i}}
to the space
\omega_{i}
, where
\omega_{i}
is a sub-representation of
(1)^{\otimes r_{i}}
. See Appendix
D

for the exact form of
P_{\omega}
.

4.5
Generalization to equivariant functions

Actually, we can always generate an equivariant function
f
from an invariant function
[
16
]
. We have

Lemma 4.5
.

Given an invariant function
f:\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{i}U_{i}\rightarrow F
with input
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{m}
in space
V_{1},\dots,V_{n},U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
, we can always construct an equivariant function
T_{\rm up}(f):\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i}\bar{U}_{i}
, where
G
acts on
\bar{U}_{i}
by the dual representation of
U_{i}
, by defining

T_{\rm up}(f)^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})=\left.\frac{\partial f(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{m})}{\partial\bm{y}_{i}}\right|_{\bm{y}_{1}=\cdots=\bm{y}_{n}=0}

(12)

where we have choose a natural set of basis for
U_{i}
and the corresponding dual basis for
\bar{U}_{i}
. Besides, any equivariant function can be obtained in this way.

We give the poof of above Lemma and more detail about obtaining equivariant function from the invariant function in Appendix
F
. From the Lemma
4.5
and tensor network calculations we can obtain the following theorem,

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
be input variables in space
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
and
\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{m}
be output variables in space
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
, each equivariant function
h:\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i}\bar{U}_{i}
can be expressed as

h^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n})=\sum_{j}q^{i}_{j}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})t^{i}_{j}

(13)

where
q^{i}_{j}
are functions,
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}\in\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
are tensor network generators of
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
, and
t_{j}^{i}
is the tensor networks (labeled by
j
) which are obtained by removing the output variables
\bm{y}_{i}
from the tensor network generators of
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{i}
, in which the
\bm{y}_{i}
variable appears exactly once.

Proof.

We classify the tensor network generators into three types

1.

G_{1}
: tensor network generators that only contain
\bm{x}_{i}

2.

G_{2}
: tensor network generators that contain exactly one
\bm{y}_{i}

3.

G_{3}
: tensor network generators that contain more than one
\bm{y}_{i}

According to Lemma 4.5

h^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})=\left.\frac{\partial f(\{\bm{g}_{u}\},\{\bar{\bm{t}}^{v}_{k}\},\{\bm{s}_{w}\})}{\partial\bm{y}_{i}}\right|_{\bar{\bm{t}}^{v}_{j}=\bm{s}_{w}=0}

(14)

where
\bm{g}_{u}\in G_{1}
,
\bar{\bm{t}}^{v}_{k}\in G_{2}
(
v
means the generator contains exactly one
\bm{y}_{v}
),
\bm{s}_{w}\in G_{3}
.

It’s easy to see that

\displaystyle h^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\left.\frac{\partial f(\{\bm{g}_{u}\},\{\bar{\bm{t}}^{v}_{k}\},\{\bm{s}_{w}\})}{\partial\bar{\bm{t}}^{i}_{j}}\right|_{\bar{\bm{t}}^{v}_{j}=\bm{w}_{i}=0}\frac{\partial\bar{\bm{t}}^{i}_{j}}{\partial\bm{y}_{i}}

(15)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}q^{i}_{j}(\{\bm{g}_{u}\})\bm{t}^{v}_{j}

(16)

where
q^{i}_{j}(\{\bm{g}_{u}\})
is a function that represents
\left.\frac{\partial f(\{\bm{g}_{u}\},\{\bar{\bm{t}}^{v}_{k}\},\{\bm{s}_{w}\})}{\partial\bar{\bm{t}}^{i}_{j}}\right|_{\bar{\bm{t}}^{v}_{j}=\bm{s}_{w}=0}
, and
\bm{t}^{i}_{j}
is
\bar{\bm{t}}^{i}_{j}
with
\bm{y}_{i}
missing.
∎

5
Constructing invariant and equivariant operations for geometry graph neural network

In this section, we will show how to use the framework developed above to construct the equivariant operations of the geometry GNN.

5.1
Geometry graph neural network

Geometry GNN are built on the graph structured data with the 3D geometric information. For the node feature
\mathbf{h}_{i}
of node
i
and
\mathbf{h}_{j}
of its neighbour
j\in\ N(i)
in layer
l
, the interaction message
\mathbf{m}_{ij}^{l+1}
for them is

\mathbf{m}_{ij}^{l+1}=f_{m}\left(\mathbf{h}_{i}^{l},\mathbf{h}_{j}^{l}\right),

(17)

where the map
f_{m}
is a learnable function. Then the interaction message
\mathbf{m}_{ij}
for all the neighbour
j\in\ N(i)
are aggregated by a permutation invariant function
\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{N}(i)}
, such as sum and mean, which is used to update the node feature
\mathbf{h}_{i}
of node
i
according to another learnable function
f_{u}
,

\mathbf{h}_{i}^{l+1}=f_{u}\left(\mathbf{h}_{i}^{l},\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{N}(i)}\mathbf{m}_{ij}^{l}\right).

(18)

For the equivariant geometry GNN, the function
f_{m}
and
f_{u}
need to be
SO(3)
equivariant function. Many of equivariant geometry GNN researches focus on design novel equivariant function
f_{m}
and
f_{u}
. The feature
\mathbf{h}_{i}
and message
\mathbf{m}_{ij}
can be cartesian tensor
[
8
]
or spherical tensor
[
6
,
7
]
.

5.2
Constructing invariant and equivariant operations for cartesian tensor feature

For cartesian tensor feature
\mathbf{h}
and message
\mathbf{m}_{ij}
, we consider the tensor rank to be 0 (scalar), 1 (vector), 2 (matrix) or higher. Here, we show how to construct the fuctions (
17
) with cartesian tensor feature and message. For simplicity, we just construct the tensor networks that
\mathbf{h}
and
\mathbf{m}_{ij}
appear at most once. More complicated operations can be constructed in the similar way.

5.2.1
Vector input and vector output

We set
\mathbf{h}_{i}=\mathbf{u}
,
\mathbf{h}_{j}=\mathbf{v}
,
\mathbf{m}_{ij}=\mathbf{w}
and
\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The invariances we can construct are

where the red lines represent the output space. Eq.(

) just is
u_{i}(v_{i})w_{j}\delta_{ij}

1

means
u_{i}w_{j}\delta_{ij}
and
v_{i}w_{j}\delta_{ij}
.
and
\varepsilon_{ijk}u_{i}v_{j}w_{k}

(20)

\mathbf{u}
(
\mathbf{v}

\mathbf{u}\times\mathbf{v}

I

6
Constructing equivariant machine learning model

In this section, we show that we can use the the framework we developed to construct
SO(3)
invariant and equivariant neural network model. For
SO(3)
invariant neural network model, we can construct the functions as follows,

f_{\rm inv}(\bm{x})=q(g_{1}(\bm{x}),\dots,g_{m}(\bm{x}))

(29)

where
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
are tensor network generators and
q
is a general neural network model, as shown in Fig.
3
.

\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{x}_{n}
\cdots
f_{\rm inv}
=
p
g_{1}
g_{m}
\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{x}_{n}
\cdots
\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{x}_{n}
\cdots
\cdots

Figure 3:
The general form of
SO(3)
invariant neural network model
f_{\rm inv}
by composing a general neural network
p
with
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
, which are tensor network generators.

In addition, according to the Lemma
4.5
and Theorem
4.6
, we can always construct an equivariant neural network model from an invariant neural network model using the transformation
T_{\text{up}}
, as shown in Fig.
4
.

f_{\rm equiv}
\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{x}_{n}
\cdots
\bm{y}_{1}
\bm{y}_{m}
\cdots
=\ T_{\rm up}
p
g_{1}
g_{m}
\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{y}_{m}
\cdots
\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{y}_{m}
\cdots
\cdots

Figure 4:
The general form of
SO(3)
equivariant neural network model by composing a general neural network
p
with
g_{1},\dots,g_{m}
, which are tensor network generators, and then applying
T_{\rm up}
.

As an application example, we use this construction methods for learning the constitutive law of materials. The constitutive law is a mathematical expression that describes how a material responds to external influences such as forces or deformations. For small deformations, the constitutive law always has the linear relationship with the deformations
[
34
,
35
]
,

\mathbf{P}=\mu\left(\mathbf{F}+\mathbf{F}^{T}-2\mathbf{I}\right)+\lambda(\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbf{F})-3)\mathbf{I},

(30)

where
\mathbf{F}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}

is the deformation tensor,
\mathbf{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
is the first Piola–Kirchhoff stress tensor,
\mu
and
\lambda
are the Lamé parameters, and
\mathbf{I}
is the identity matrix. For large deformations, the constitutive law may have the nonlinear term, such as Neo-Hookean model
[
34
,
35
]
,

\mathbf{P}=\mu\left(\mathbf{FF}^{T}-\mathbf{I}\right)+\lambda\log(\operatorname{det}(\mathbf{F}))\mathbf{I}.

(31)

To obtain the constitutive law which match the experimental data, researchers often use the data driven method, such as polynomial fitting
[
36
,
37
]
. Here, we construct the
SO(3)
equivariant neural network model to predict the cartesian tensor
\mathbf{P}
from the cartesian tensor
\mathbf{F}
. Using the aforementioned methods, we first construct
invariant operations:
\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F})
,
\text{Tr}(\mathbf{FF}^{T})
,
\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F}^{2})
,
\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F}^{3})
,
\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F}^{2}\mathbf{F}^{T})
. And we can also construct the equivariant operations:
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}
,
\mathbf{FF}
,
\mathbf{FF}^{T}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{F}^{T}\mathbf{F}^{T}
. Hence, the neural network model to be learned can be choose as

\mathbf{P}=f(\mathbf{F})=\sum_{i}^{n}h_{i}(\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F}),\text{Tr}(\mathbf{FF}^{T}),\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F}^{2}),\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F}^{3}),\text{Tr}(\mathbf{F}^{2}\mathbf{F}^{T}))\mathbf{E}_{i}

(32)

where
\mathbf{E}_{i}
is the equivariant operations, and
h_{i}
is a common neural network with
n
outputs, for which we use muti-layer perceptrons (MLP) in this work.

We use the Eq.(
31
) to generate the synthetic dataset (see the details of the experimental setting in Appendix
H
). We use MLP, equivariant function Eq.(
32
) with 3 equivariant features (
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}
) and equivariant function Eq.(
32
) with 7 equivariant features (
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}
,
\mathbf{FF}
,
\mathbf{FF}^{T}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}\mathbf{F}^{T}
) to learn from the training data with different size. As shown in Fig.
5
, we can see the test mean squared error (MSE) of the equivariant function with 7 equivariant features is several ranks of magnitude lower than the results of MLP for same training size, which demonstrate the advantages of using equivariant models.

Figure 5:
The test MSE as the function of training data size for MLP, equivariant model with 3 equivariant features (
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}
) and equivariant model with 7 equivariant features (
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}
,
\mathbf{FF}
,
\mathbf{FF}^{T}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}\mathbf{F}
,
\mathbf{F}^{T}\mathbf{F}^{T}
), respectively. The error bars represent the standard deviation calculated from three runs.

7
Conclusion

This work introduces a general framework for constructing invariant and equivariant operations, designed to handle various data forms, including tuples of Cartesian tensors of different ranks and spherical tensors of different types. This framework is particularly useful for building symmetric operations within geometric graph neural networks. More broadly, it can be applied to any machine learning model that requires symmetry constraints, by first generating fundamental equivariant and invariant quantities which can then be leveraged by conventional neural networks for learning. In future work, we can utilize low-rank, structured tensor networks to parameterize complex equivariant operations, thereby improving sample efficiency and reducing computational complexity.

Appendix

Appendix A
Symmetric tensor networks

The contraction of a tensor network yields a single resultant tensor. When the contracted tensor is constrained by a specific global symmetry, we can assume that every constituent tensor in the original network also satisfies the same symmetry condition, which is termed as symmetric tensors
[
9
,
10
,
11
]
. This deduction is justified by the invariant property that the contraction operation preserves the symmetry characteristics of symmetric tensors.

The vector space
V
associated with each index of a symmetric tensor constitutes a representation space of the group, which admits the following decomposition:

V\cong\bigoplus_{s}d_{s}V_{s}\cong\bigoplus_{s}(D_{s}\otimes V_{s}),

(33)

where
V_{s}
is the irreducible representation space for the
s
-th representation of the group,
d_{s}
denotes the multiplicity of this representation, and
D_{s}
stands for the
d_{s}
-dimensional degeneracy space.

The basis for the
r
-th index of the tensor can be constructed using the decomposition described in Eq. (
33
). In the decomposition of the representation space
V=\bigoplus_{s}(D_{s}\otimes V_{s})
for some index, the basis vectors are parameterized by the triplet
(s,\alpha_{s},m_{s})
, where

s
labels the irreducible representations of the group,

\alpha_{s}=0,1,\cdots d_{s}-1
indexes the degeneracy space
D_{s}
of dimension
d_{s}
, and

m_{s}
corresponds to the internal basis of the irreducible representation space
V_{s}
.

Under this basis, a tensor satisfies the symmetry condition if it meets the following criteria at each rank:

•

Zeroth-rank (Scalar):

A scalar is trivially a symmetric tensor.

•

First-rank:

Non-zero elements must reside in the trivial irreducible representation (
s=0
).

•

Second-rank:

Non-zero elements require both indices to belong to the conjugate irreducible representation space.
The tensor must take the form:

T_{(s_{0},\alpha_{s_{0}},m_{s_{0}}),(s_{1},\alpha_{s_{1}},m_{s_{1}})}=P_{(s_{0},\alpha_{s_{0}}),(s_{1},\alpha_{s_{1}})}\delta_{s_{0},s_{1}}\begin{pmatrix}s_{0}\\
m_{s_{0}}\quad m_{s_{1}}\end{pmatrix},

(34)

where the brackets denote the Wigner 1-jm symbol
[
38
]
.

•

Third-rank:

The tensor must satisfy the following structure:

T_{(s_{0},\alpha_{s_{0}},m_{s_{0}}),(s_{1},\alpha_{s_{1}},m_{s_{1}}),(s_{2},\alpha_{s_{2}},m_{s_{2}})}=P_{(s_{0},\alpha_{s_{0}}),(s_{1},\alpha_{s_{1}}),(s_{2},\alpha_{s_{2}})}\begin{pmatrix}s_{0}&s_{1}&s_{2}\\
m_{0}&m_{1}&m_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(35)

with the brackets representing the Wigner 3-jm symbol
[
38
]
.

•

Higher-rank:

The tensor must be decomposable into contractions of multiple third-rank or lower-rank symmetric tensors.

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem 4.3

Proof of Theorem 4.3.

It’s easy to see that each invariant polynomial is a finite sum of homogeneous invariant polynomials. Therefore we only need to study homogeneous invariant polynomials. Let
p
be a homogeneous invariant polynomial. Then we can write

as a tensor network contraction.

(36)

where multiplicity of
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
is allowed. Since
p
is invariant, for
g\in SO(3)
we have

p(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n})=p(U(g)^{\otimes r_{1}}\bm{x}_{1},\dots,U(g)^{\otimes r_{n}}\bm{x}_{n})

(37)

U
is the 3D representations of
SO(3)
and
r_{i}
is the rank of
\bm{x}_{i}
. That is,

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\hbox to171.97pt{\vbox to57.57pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.98643pt\lower-49.18436pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -15.75 0 L -15.75 -59.06}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

}}}}}}$}

(39)

where
P_{\rm sym}
is the projection to symmetric subspace under permutation within identical
\bm{x}_{i}
s.

{P_{\rm sym}}^{p_{1,1}\dots p_{t_{i},r_{i}}}_{q_{1,1}\dots q_{t_{i},r_{i}}}=\frac{1}{t_{i}!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{t_{i}}}\prod_{j}\prod_{k}\delta_{q_{\sigma(j),k}}^{p_{j,k}}

(40)

where
t_{i}
is the multiplicity of
x_{i}
, and
\sigma
take value in all permutation of
t_{i}
elements.

It’s easy to see that
P_{\rm sym}
commutes with
U^{\otimes r_{i}t_{i}}

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{
\hbox to171.45pt{\vbox to79.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 85.72458pt\lower-71.3319pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -110.24 0 L -110.24 -98.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -78.74 0 L -78.74 -98.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}

(42)

Combining Eq.(
39
) and Eq.(
41
), it’s easy to see that
T^{\prime}_{p}
is an
SO(3)
-symmetric tensor. By Lemma 4.2,
T^{\prime}_{p}
is linear combination of tensor product of delta tensors and at most one Levi-Civita tensor. For polynomial
p(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n})
, we can always use the partially permutation symmetrization of the tensor
T_{p}
as its coefficients, that is,

(43)

Therefore
p(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n})
is linear combination of contraction of tensor network formed by
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
(multiplicity is allowed) together with at most one Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{ijk}
. Since contraction of disconnected tensor network is product of the contraction of each component,
\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
is generated by the contraction of connected tensor network formed by
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
(multiplicity is allowed) together with at most one Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{ijk}
.
∎

Appendix C
Decomposition of tensor product representation
(1)^{\otimes l}

We have

(1)^{\otimes l}=(l)^{d_{l,l}}\oplus(l-1)^{d_{l,l-1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus(s)^{d_{l,s}}\oplus\cdots\oplus(0)^{d_{l,0}}

(44)

By counting the dimension of
L_{z}
eigen-spaces
3
3

3

L_{x},L_{y},L_{z}
is the orbital angular momentum operator in x, y, z direction, respectively
[
39
]
.
, we have

d_{l,s}=\sum_{i=s}^{\left\lfloor\frac{s+l}{2}\right\rfloor}\frac{l!}{i!(s+l-2i)!(i-s)!}-\sum_{i=s+1}^{\left\lfloor\frac{s+l+1}{2}\right\rfloor}\frac{l!}{i!(s+l+1-2i)!(i-s-1)!}.

(45)

Particularly,
d_{l,l}=1
,
d_{l,l-1}=l-1
and
d_{l,l-2}=\frac{l(l-1)}{2}
. When
l
is small, the exact decomposition is listed below

\displaystyle(1)^{\otimes 2}

\displaystyle=(2)\oplus(1)\oplus(0)

(46)

\displaystyle(1)^{\otimes 3}

\displaystyle=(3)\oplus(2)^{2}\oplus(1)^{3}\oplus(0)

(47)

\displaystyle(1)^{\otimes 4}

\displaystyle=(4)\oplus(3)^{3}\oplus(2)^{6}\oplus(1)^{6}\oplus(0)^{3}

(48)

\displaystyle(1)^{\otimes 5}

\displaystyle=(5)\oplus(4)^{4}\oplus(3)^{10}\oplus(2)^{15}\oplus(1)^{15}\oplus(0)^{6}

(49)

Appendix D
The exact form of projector
P_{l}
and
P_{\omega}

For
l
representation, we have basis vector
|m\rangle
(
-l\leq m\leq l
). The
l
sub-representation in
(1)^{\otimes l}
satisfies

\sqrt{\frac{(2l)!(l-m)!}{(l+m)!}}|m\rangle=(L_{-})^{l-m}|l\rangle=\left(\sum_{i}L_{-,i}\right)^{l-m}|1,\dots,1\rangle

(50)

where
L_{\pm}=L_{x}\pm iL_{y}
is the ladder operator.
4
4

4

L_{-}|m\rangle=\sqrt{(l-m)(l+m+1)}|m-1\rangle
and
L_{-}|-l\rangle=0
, where
|m\rangle
is the standard basis of
l
representation. Especially, for 1 representation,
L_{-}|1\rangle=\sqrt{2}|0\rangle
,
L_{-}|0\rangle=\sqrt{2}|-1\rangle
and
L_{-}|-1\rangle=0

[
39
]
.
.

Then

\displaystyle|m\rangle

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{(l+m)!}{(2l)!(l-m)!}}(\sqrt{2})^{l-m}\sum_{(s_{i}),\sum s_{i}=m}\frac{(l-m)!}{2^{d(s)}}|s_{1},\dots,s_{l}\rangle

(51)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{(l-m)!(l+m)!2^{l-m}}{(2l)!}}\sum_{(s_{i}),\sum s_{i}=m}\frac{1}{2^{d(s)}}|s_{1},\dots,s_{l}\rangle

(52)

where
s_{i}=0,\pm 1
and
d(s)
is the number of
-1
in
(s)=(s_{1},\dots,s_{l})
.

Therefore

(P_{l})^{(s)}_{m}=\frac{1}{2^{d(s)}}\sqrt{\frac{(l-m)!(l+m)!2^{l-m}}{(2l)!}}\delta_{\sum s_{i},m}

(53)

To get
P_{\omega}
, we need to construct the orthogonal matrix
O_{l}
of the transformation
(1)^{\otimes l}\rightarrow(l_{1})\oplus(l_{2})\oplus\cdots
. If we define
Q_{l}
as the orthogonal matrix of the transformation
(l)\otimes(1)\rightarrow(l+1)\oplus(l)\oplus(l-1)
. We can infer
O_{l}
by induction:

\displaystyle O_{1}

\displaystyle=Q_{1}

(54)

O_{l+1}
\cdots

\displaystyle=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{

(58)

is
SO(3)
-symmetric and the free legs are of 3D representation. By Lemma 4.2, the contraction of this tensor network is linear combination of tensor product of delta tensors and at most one Levi-Civita tensor. Therefore
p(\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n})
is linear combination of contraction of tensor network formed by
P_{l_{1}}(\bm{x}_{1}),\dots,P_{l_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
(multiplicity is allowed) together with at most one Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{ijk}
. Since contraction of disconnected tensor network is product of the contraction of each component,
\mathbb{R}[V]^{SO(3)}
is generated by the contraction of connected tensor network formed by
P_{l_{1}}(\bm{x}_{1}),\dots,P_{l_{n}}(\bm{x}_{n})
(multiplicity is allowed) together with at most one Levi-Civita tensor
\epsilon_{ijk}
.
∎

Appendix F
Obtaining the equivariant functions from the invarant functions

We can give the proof of Lemma 4.5 as following:

Proof of Lemma 4.5.

Since
f
is invariant, for each
g\in G
,

\displaystyle T_{\rm up}(f)^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})_{\alpha}=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial f(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{1},\cdots,\bm{y}_{m})}{\partial(\bm{y}_{i})_{\alpha}}\right|_{\bm{y}_{1}=\cdots=\bm{y}_{n}=0}

(59)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial f(g\cdot\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,g\cdot\bm{x}_{n},g\cdot\bm{y}_{1},\cdots,g\cdot\bm{y}_{m})}{\partial(\bm{y}_{i})_{\alpha}}\right|_{\bm{y}_{1}=\cdots=\bm{y}_{n}=0}

(60)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}\left.\frac{\partial f(g\cdot\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,g\cdot\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\bm{y}^{\prime}_{m})}{\partial(\bm{y}^{\prime}_{i})_{\beta}}\right|_{\bm{y}^{\prime}_{1}=\cdots=\bm{y}^{\prime}_{n}=0}\frac{\partial(\bm{y}^{\prime}_{i})_{\beta}}{\partial(\bm{y}_{i})_{\alpha}}

(61)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}T_{\rm up}(f)^{i}(g\cdot\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,g\cdot\bm{x}_{n})_{\beta}\rho_{i}(g)_{\beta\alpha}

(62)

where
\bm{y}^{\prime}_{i}=g\cdot\bm{y}_{i}
.

Therefore

\displaystyle T_{\rm up}(f)^{i}(g\cdot\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,g\cdot\bm{x}_{n})_{\alpha}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}T_{\rm up}(f)^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})_{\beta}\rho_{i}(g^{-1})_{\beta\alpha}

(63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}\bar{\rho}_{i}(g)_{\alpha\beta}T_{\rm up}(f)^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n})_{\beta}

(64)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(g\cdot T_{\rm up}(f)^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n}))_{\alpha}

(65)

T_{\rm up}(f)
is equivariant.

To prove that any equivariant function can be obtained in this way, we consider the following construction. Given an equivariant function
f:\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{i}U_{i}
with input
\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{n}
in space
V_{1},\dots,V_{n}
and output
\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{m}
in space
U_{1},\dots,U_{m}
, we can construct an invariant function
T_{\rm down}(f):\bigoplus_{i}V_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{i}\bar{U}_{i}\rightarrow F
, where
G
acts on
\bar{U}_{i}
by the dual representation of
U_{i}
, by defining

T_{\rm down}(f)(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n},\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{m})=\sum_{i}\left\langle f^{i}(\bm{x}_{1},\cdots,\bm{x}_{n}),\bm{y}_{i}\right\rangle

(66)

where we have choose a natural set of basis for
U_{i}
and the corresponding dual basis for
\bar{U}_{i}
,
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
denotes the natural function
U_{i}\times\bar{U}_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

By simple deduction, one can show that
T_{\rm up}\circ T_{\rm down}(f)=f
. Therefore, any equivariant function
f
can be constructed by
T_{\rm up}(h)
where
h=T_{\rm down}(f)
.
∎

Notice that for
SU(2)
and
SO(3)
, each representation is similar to its dual.
The transformation
T_{\rm up}
and
T_{\rm down}
can be pictorially expressed by Fig.
6
.

f
\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{x}_{n}
\bm{y}_{m}
\bm{y}_{1}
\cdots
\cdots
\overset{T_{\rm up}}{\longrightarrow}
\underset{T_{\rm down}}{\longleftarrow}
g
\bm{x}_{1}
\bm{x}_{n}
\cdots
\bm{y}_{1}
\bm{y}_{m}
\cdots

Figure 6:
T_{\rm up}
transforms an invariant function into an equivariant function.
T_{\rm down}
transforms an equivariant function into an invariant function.

Appendix G
Represent spherical tensor equivariant function by tensor network

The TP operations can be expressed by

\bm{c}_{t}=\sum_{rs}M_{rst}\bm{a}_{r}\bm{b}_{s}

(67)

where the type of
\bm{a}
,
\bm{b}
and
\bm{c}
are
l_{a}
,
l_{b}
and
l_{c}
respectively,
1\leq r\leq 2l_{a}+1,1\leq s\leq 2l_{b}+1,1\leq t\leq 2l_{c}+1
and
M
is a rank-3 symmetric tensor, whose indices are of representation
l_{a},l_{b}
and
l_{c}
.

By theory of symmetric tensor in appendix
A
,
M_{rst}
proportional
C_{rst}
, which is the unique symmetric projection tensor
(l_{a})\otimes(l_{b})\rightarrow(l_{c})
(also called CG coefficients). In other words, we have

\bm{c}_{t}=\sum_{rs}AC_{rst}\bm{a}_{r}\bm{b}_{s}

(68)

Following the constructing method of Theorem 4.4, we have

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}}}}$}.

(70)

To prove that our construct is equivalent to the TP operation, we only need to prove that
C^{\prime}_{rst}
is non-zero (obviously
C^{\prime}_{rst}=1
when
r,s,t
are of the highest weight) and is proportional to
C_{rst}
by a factor independent of
\bm{a},\bm{b},\bm{c}
, which is clear since
C^{\prime}_{rst}
and
C_{rst}
are both symmetric rank-3 tensor with indices of representation
(l_{a}),(l_{b}),(l_{c})
, and the symmetric rank-3 tensor with indices of representation
(l_{a}),(l_{b}),(l_{c})
is unique up to rescaling.

Appendix H
Details of the experimental setting

We use the Eq.(25) of the main paper to generate the synthetic dataset, following the way of work
[
34
]
. The sizes of the training set are 100, 500, 1000, 2000, 5000 and 10000. The sizes of the test set and validation set are both 1000. The optimizer we choose is Adam. The learning rate is
5\times 10^{-4}
and weight decay is
1\times 10^{-8}
. We also use a cosine annealing schedule during the training. We use one CPU (Intel i7-13700K) for the numerical experiments. The total time consuming of training the three model with different training size is about 36 minutes.
